| 1024 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I think we should have longer breaks because we need time to relax. Today, Priya stands in the middle of the circle with a big sheet of paper. She did a survey! She asked all twenty council children five questions about school rules. Of course, the survey has no names, so nobody had to share private information. Priya says, Here are the results. Most students want to choose their own seats. Say it with me. Most students want to choose their own seats. Great! Most means more than half. When we talk about numbers, we can also say, Fifteen out of twenty. Let's read Priya's survey results. "School Rules Survey. Twenty council children answered. Fifteen out of twenty want to choose their own seats in class. Twelve want longer breaks. Eight think students should wear uniforms, and twelve do not. Seventeen think phones should stay in our bags during lessons. Only a few students, just two, want more homework! Thank you for answering. Your answers help us understand each other." How many children want to choose their own seats? Fifteen out of twenty. How many want more homework? Only two. Leo laughs and says, I'm one of the two! I like homework because it helps me remember. Priya says, That's a fact I didn't know about you! Then Kiko raises her hand. She says, I partly agree with choosing seats, but some people might feel left out. Olive rings her bell gently. She says, That's an important point, Kiko. Rules should be fair for everyone. Now, phonics. Look at the word before ity. Major becomes majority. The majority means more than half. Popular becomes popularity. Responsible changes ble to bil and becomes responsibility. Say the part before ity strongly. ma-JOR-i-ty, pop-u-LAR-i-ty, re-spon-si-BIL-i-ty. Next time, we look at both sides of the uniform question again, and something golden may happen! | Hello, everyone! | 1025 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Most students want to choose their own seats. Today is our big school rules discussion. Olive rings her bell and says, Let's think about uniforms one more time. This time, let's look at both sides together. Ms. Emma holds out her right hand and says, On the one hand（いっぽうでは）, uniforms save time. Then she holds out her left hand and says, On the other hand, students can't choose their clothes. Say it with me. On the one hand, uniforms save time. On the other hand, students can't choose their clothes. Fantastic! This is a great way to show two points of view. Every idea has advantages and disadvantages. Birimo asks, What do you mean by save time? Leo answers, I mean we don't have to choose clothes every morning. Then Priya writes a summary on the big board. Let's read the summary. "Our School Rules Discussion. Uniforms: On the one hand, they save time, and everyone looks equal. On the other hand, students can't choose their clothes, and uniforms can cost a lot. Longer breaks: On the one hand, they help us relax and concentrate. On the other hand, school may finish later. Choosing seats: Most students like it, but nobody should feel left out. Thank you for listening to each other!" What is one disadvantage of uniforms? They can cost a lot. What is one advantage of longer breaks? They help us relax. Olive looks around the circle. She says, You shared your ideas kindly, and you listened so well. Suddenly, the Tree of Voices begins to shine. On the first empty branch, a golden leaf grows! Everyone cheers. Now, phonics. Let's review ity words. Equal becomes equality. Responsible changes ble to bil and becomes responsibility. Major becomes majority. Say the part before ity strongly. e-QUAL-i-ty, re-spon-si-BIL-i-ty, ma-JOR-i-ty. Next time, Amara tells us about a problem at break time. Is it fair or not fair? | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Most students want to choose their own seats. | 1028 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Everyone should have a chance to try. Today, Olive brings a picture book from the Hall's library. She says, Sometimes we see someone being unkind. What can we do? Let's think about it together. First, Ms. Emma tells us three important things. Never fight, and never laugh along. Stand up for your friend with calm, kind words. And always tell a teacher or another adult right away. You can say, Stop it. That's not kind. Say it with me. Stop it. That's not kind. Well done! Say it calmly, not angrily. Let's read the story. "Tom loves drawing. One day, Mia picks up his picture and laughs. Your dog looks like a potato, she says, and she teases him in front of everyone. Tom looks down. His friend Sam stands up and says calmly, Stop it. That's not kind. Then Sam stands next to Tom and asks, Are you OK? I'll stay with you. Together, they tell their teacher right away. The next day, Mia comes to Tom and says, I'm sorry. I was wrong. Tom accepts her apology, and they draw a dog together." What does Sam say to Mia? Stop it. That's not kind. What does Mia do the next day? She says sorry. Priya says, I think telling a teacher is the most important part because adults can help. Amara says, I partly agree. Staying with Tom is important, too, because then he doesn't feel scared. Leo says, I like the ending. People can change. Olive nods and says, Different people can coexist when we are kind to each other. Now, phonics. Inter means between, or with each other. The internet is networks connected with each other. Co means together. A coworker works together with you. Coexist means live together. Next time, we will learn how to invite someone to join us at break time! | Hello, everyone! | 1029 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Stop it. That's not kind. Today, there is a short break between council meetings. Olive calls it an intermission. We go out to the Hall's garden with Ms. Emma and play a ball game with Leo and Amara. Then Kiko sees a new council child named Ken. He is sitting by himself on a bench, looking at a book. Kiko walks over with a smile. She says, Hi, Ken. Do you want to join us? Say it with me. Do you want to join us? Lovely! Ken looks up and says, Sure! Thank you for asking. He kicks the ball, and everyone cheers. Later, Amara shows us a poster she made with Priya. Let's read the poster. "How to Include Everyone. Look around at break time. Is anyone sitting by themselves? Walk over, smile, and say, Do you want to join us? Use their name if you know it. Some people may want quiet time, and that is OK, too. You can say, Maybe next time! Nobody should feel left out. A small question can make a big friend." What can you say to invite someone? Do you want to join us? What if someone wants quiet time? That's OK. Say, Maybe next time. Haru says, I think we should always invite people because nobody likes to feel left out. Riko says, I partly agree. But some people really like reading quietly, so we should ask first and listen to the answer. Ken laughs. He says, Today I wanted to play. But tomorrow, I might want to finish my book! Amara and Priya are the coauthors of the poster, because they wrote it together. Now, phonics. Inter means between. An intermission is a break between parts of a show. Co means together. A coauthor writes a book together with someone. A costar is a star of a movie together with another star. Next time, we will start a Kindness Campaign! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Stop it. | 1030 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Do you want to join us? Today is a special day. Olive rings her bell and says, This week, we talked about fairness and kindness. Now, let's share what we learned with the whole Hall. It's time for our Kindness Campaign! We work in groups with Leo, Amara, Priya, and Ken. We cooperate, and everyone uses their own ability. Amara draws pictures, Priya checks the spelling, and Leo thinks of the slogan. Haru stands up with the poster and says, Let's make our school a kind place. Say it with me. Let's make our school a kind place. Beautiful! Then the four friends give their presentation. Let's read the poster. "Kindness Campaign. Let's make our school a kind place! First, say hello to everyone, even people you don't know yet. Second, take turns and share, so everyone has a fair chance. Finally, invite people to play, and listen if they want quiet time. If you see someone being unkind, say calmly, Stop it, and tell a teacher right away. Kind words make a happy school!" What is the second idea? Take turns and share. What should you do if you see someone being unkind? Say, Stop it, and tell a teacher. At the end, Kiko says, Thank you for listening. Do you have any questions? Priya asks, Which idea is the most important? Kiko says, I think inviting people is the most important because nobody feels left out. Haru says, I partly agree, but saying hello is the easiest way to start. Olive says, You listened to each other so well. Then the Tree of Voices shines again, and a second golden leaf grows! Now, phonics. Let's review. International. Inter means between, so it means between nations. Cooperate. Co means together, so it means work together. Ability. Able changes ble to bil and adds ity. a-BIL-i-ty. Next time, Olive will tell us about something very important that many countries agreed on. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Do you want to join us? Today is a special day. Olive rings her bell and says, This week, we talked about fairness and kindness. Now, let's share what we learned with the whole Hall. It's time for our Kindness Campaign! We work in groups with Leo, Amara, Priya, and Ken. We cooperate, and everyone uses their own ability. Amara draws pictures, Priya checks the spelling, and Leo thinks of the slogan. Haru stands up with the poster and says, Let's make our school a kind place. Say it with me. Let's make our school a kind place. Beautiful! Then the four friends give their presentation. Let's read the poster. "Kindness Campaign. Let's make our school a kind place! First, say hello to everyone, even people you don't know yet. Second, take turns and share, so everyone has a fair chance. Finally, invite people to play, and listen if they want quiet time. If you see someone being unkind, say calmly, Stop it, and tell a teacher right away. Kind words make a happy school!" What is the second idea? Take turns and share. What should you do if you see someone being unkind? Say, Stop it, and tell a teacher. At the end, Kiko says, Thank you for listening. Do you have any questions? Priya asks, Which idea is the most important? Kiko says, I think inviting people is the most important because nobody feels left out. Haru says, I partly agree, but saying hello is the easiest way to start. Olive says, You listened to each other so well. Then the Tree of Voices shines again, and a second golden leaf grows! Now, phonics. Let's review. International. Inter means between, so it means between nations. Cooperate. Co means together, so it means work together. Ability. Able changes b, l, e to b, i, l and adds i, t, y. a-BIL-i-ty. Next time, Olive will tell us about something very important that many countries agreed on. | B+C | bare 'ble' / 'bil' / 'ity' are misread by TTS; spell the letters |

Then Ms. Emma finds a notice on the board by the door. Let's read the notice. "Willowford Park Clean-Up Day. Join us on Saturday afternoon from one to three! Bring a hat and a water bottle. We will give you gloves, tongs, and bags. Children must come with an adult. Last spring, our volunteers picked up forty bags of litter and planted sunflowers near the pond. Let's make our park clean and beautiful again. Thank you, Willowford!" When is the clean-up? On Saturday afternoon, from one to three. What did the volunteers plant last spring? Sunflowers. Kiko says, Why don't we join the clean-up? Ms. Emma says, Good idea, but we'll all go together with the adults. Now, phonics. Listen to the strong new words. Did you plant roses? | 1035 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, What can children do for our community? On Saturday afternoon, we cleaned Willowford Park with Ms. Emma and the other adults, and we said hello to so many neighbors. Today is a special day. Each group will give a short talk about rights and responsibilities. Priya interviews Leo about safety at school. Amara talks about her pets. Then it's our turn. Riko stands up and says, I'd like to talk about rights and responsibilities. Olive says, Go ahead. We're listening. Riko's big idea is this. We have the right to speak, and we should listen to others. Say it with me. We have the right to speak, and we should listen to others. Beautiful! Let's read Riko's speech. "Hello, everyone. Children have many rights. We have the right to learn, so we should try our best at school. We have the right to play, so we should share the ball and take turns. We have the right to be safe, so we should help keep our town clean and safe. And we have the right to speak, and we should listen to others. Every voice matters, so every ear matters, too. Thank you!" What should we do because we have the right to play? Share the ball and take turns. Why does every ear matter? Because every voice matters. The audience claps, and Olive rings her bell. Then the Tree of Voices begins to glow. A third golden leaf slowly grows on its branch! Now, phonics. Let's review. Do you like that one? No, I like THIS one. Interview. Inter means each other, so you see each other and talk. Coexist. Co means together, so coexist means live together. Security. Secure, drop the e, and add ity. Say it strongly, se-CU-ri-ty. | 1036 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We have the right to speak, and we should listen to others. And the third golden leaf is shining on the Tree of Voices! This morning, a special guest walks into the round meeting hall. She has a big smile and a red scarf. It's Mayor Hart, the mayor of Willowford! She shakes hands with everyone and gives Olive a letter. Let's read Mayor Hart's letter. "Dear Children's Council, I am happy to inform you that the town of Willowford would like to receive your ideas. Children see things that adults sometimes miss. We request your help with one question: How can we make Willowford better? Please share your suggestions with me next week. Every good idea is welcome, big or small. With warm wishes, Mayor Hart." What does Mayor Hart want to know? How we can make Willowford better. When should the council share their ideas? Next week. Kiko thinks about the station. It's a little gray and plain. She raises her hand and says, I suggest that we plant flowers at the station. Say it with me. I suggest that we plant flowers at the station. Lovely! The words I suggest that we are polite and a little formal. After it, just put the verb: I suggest that we plant, I suggest that we clean. With friends, you can also say, How about planting flowers? or Why don't we plant flowers? Haru says, That's a good idea, but who will water them? Riko answers, Why don't we ask the station staff to help? Now, phonics. In letters and notices, we often use formal words. Kids becomes children. Get becomes receive. Remember, after c, write e before i: r, e, c, e, i, v, e. Ask for becomes request. Both kinds of words are correct English. Next time, we will share even more ideas for Willowford! | Hello, everyone! | 1038 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, How about making a map for visitors? Today, Olive takes us outside, with Ms. Emma, to the empty lot next to the Hall of Wisdom. It's just dirt and a few old stones now, and the old fence needs to be repaired. Mayor Hart wants to know what the children would like here. Leo says, I think it should be a playground, because children need a place to run and play. Amara says, I see your point, but I think it should be a flower garden. Flowers make people happy, and bees need them, too. Leo and Amara are both right, so what can we do? Olive says, When two people want different things, we can look for a compromise（だきょうあん）. A compromise is an idea where both sides get some of what they want. Let's find a compromise. Say it with me. Let's find a compromise. Great job! Let's read Olive's notes on the board. "The Empty Lot. Leo's idea: a playground. Good points: children can run, climb, and play together. Amara's idea: a flower garden. Good points: it is beautiful, quiet, and good for bees. Our compromise: perhaps we can use both halves of the lot. A flower garden on one side and a small playground on the other side. It will require some benches and a path in the middle, so families can enjoy both." What is good about a flower garden? It is beautiful, quiet, and good for bees. What will be in the middle? Some benches and a path. Kiko says, What if we make a flower garden with a small playground? Leo and Amara smile. That sounds fair! Now, phonics. Maybe becomes perhaps. Need becomes require. R, e, q, u, i, r, e. Fix becomes repair. These formal words are common on notices. Next time, the council will vote on which idea to present to Mayor Hart! | Hello, everyone! | 1039 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's find a compromise. Leo and Amara were so happy with the garden and the small playground. Now, the council has many ideas, but Mayor Hart is coming back tomorrow, and we can present only one proposal. How can we choose fairly? Olive says, Let's vote! There are twenty children on the council. Each child writes one idea on a card and puts it in the ballot box. Then Riko and Leo count the votes, and Kiko writes the results on the board. Let's read the results. "Children's Council Vote Results. Twenty children voted today. The visitor map got twelve votes. The flowers at the station got five votes. A new bench by the pond got three votes. Twelve out of twenty is more than half, so the majority voted for the visitor map. We will present the map idea to Mayor Hart tomorrow. Thank you, everyone, for sharing your ideas and voting kindly." How many votes did the bench get? Three. Did the majority vote for the map? Yes, because twelve out of twenty is more than half. Haru reports the result. Twelve people voted for the idea. Say it with me. Twelve people voted for the idea. Perfect! Kiko voted for the flowers. She says, I'm a little sad, but the map is a great idea, too. Olive says, Thank you, Kiko. Accepting the result is a big part of voting. And perhaps the flowers can be our next idea! Now, phonics. More formal words. Tell becomes inform. Bike becomes bicycle. Bi means two, and cycle means wheel, so a bicycle has two wheels. Photo becomes photograph. P, h makes the f sound. Next time, we will present our proposal to Mayor Hart in the great hall! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Let's find a compromise. It will help people find their way. Say it with me. Our proposal is to make a map for visitors. It will help people find their way. Excellent! Let's read our proposal. "The Children's Proposal for Willowford. Many visitors get lost when they arrive at Willowford Station. Our proposal is to make a free map for visitors. It will show the library, the park, the Hall of Wisdom, and the new garden. The map will have pictures, so even small children can read it. We also suggest that the town put a big map board outside the station. Thank you for listening." Why will the map have pictures? So even small children can read it. Where should the big map board go? | 1041 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our proposal is to make a map for visitors. It will help people find their way. Mayor Hart loved our map idea, and four golden leaves are shining on the Tree of Voices. Today's council topic is animals. Amara raises her hand. She says, I saw a baby fox in a video. It was so cute! I want one as a pet! Leo says, Hmm, but a fox is a wild animal. So Olive writes a big question on the board. Should people keep wild animals as pets? Say it with me. Should people keep wild animals as pets? Great! Olive says, Let's listen to both sides before we decide. Let's read the fact card from the Hall's library. "Wild animals usually live in nature, like foxes, owls and monkeys. They need lots of space, special food, and other animals of their own kind. Many places have laws that protect wild animals. Keeping them at home can be bad for the animal and unsafe for people. Dogs, cats, rabbits and hamsters are animals that live well with people. Some wild animals are cared for by experts in zoos and sanctuaries." What do wild animals need? Lots of space, special food, and friends of their kind. Listen. A baby fox is the cutest animal in the world. Is that a fact or an opinion? It's an opinion! Amara thinks and says, I partly agree with Leo. Maybe I can draw foxes and visit them at a sanctuary with my family. Olive says, On the one hand, people love animals. On the other hand, wild animals need lots of space. Now, phonics.Spect means look. In plus spect is inspect. To inspect means to look inside carefully. Dict means say. Pre means before, so predict means to say what will happen before it happens. Struct means build. A structure is something built, like a bridge. Next time, we will talk about using less plastic! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Our proposal is to make a map for visitors. | 1042 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Should people keep wild animals as pets? Amara decided to draw foxes instead. Today, at lunchtime, Priya comes into the round hall with a big blue box. It's the recycling bin from the Hall's kitchen. She says, Yesterday, I counted the plastic bottles in this bin. There were thirty-one! Haru says, Wow, in only one day! Olive rings her bell. Let's make a suggestion. Remember, we can say, I suggest that we, and then a verb. Riko raises her hand. I suggest that we bring our own bottles. Say it with me. I suggest that we bring our own bottles. Excellent! Kiko adds, How about using reusable bags, too? Priya made a poster about plastic. Let's read Priya's poster. "Did you know? Plastic is strong and useful, but it can take hundreds of years to break down. A lot of plastic waste ends up in the sea, and it can hurt sea animals like turtles and seabirds. Here are three easy ideas. Bring your own bottle and fill it with water. Carry a reusable bag when you go shopping. Say no, thank you, to straws you don't need. Small steps can make a big difference!" How long can plastic take to break down? Hundreds of years. Which animals can plastic hurt? Sea animals, like turtles and seabirds. Then Haru says, I partly agree. Reusable bottles are great, but sometimes I forget mine. Riko says, I see your point. Why don't we put a bottle sticker on the door to remind us? Now, phonics. More roots! Port means carry. Ex means out, so export means to carry things out to another country. Rupt means break. Inter means between, so interrupt means to break in when someone is talking. Ject means throw. Re means back, so reject means to throw something back, or to say no to it. Next time, we will review how we share opinions! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Should people keep wild animals as pets? Amara decided to draw foxes instead. | 1043 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I suggest that we bring our own bottles. This morning, almost everyone came with their own bottle! Ms. Emma says, Stage 4 is almost over. Let's look back at how we share opinions. In Stage 4, we learned to give an opinion with a reason and an example. We also learned to disagree politely. Olive says, Good listeners make good speakers. Then she starts Opinion Ping-Pong. One person shares an opinion, and the next answers kindly. Birimo jumps up and says, I'm playing a boy who wants a pet monkey! A monkey can live in my room! Kiko answers, I see your point, but I think wild animals should live in nature. Say it with me. I see your point, but I think wild animals should live in nature. Perfect! Birimo asks, What do you mean by nature? Kiko says, I mean forests, rivers and the sea. Haru adds, I think so, too. For example, a monkey needs tall trees and its family. Let's read Olive's card for good speakers. "How to Share an Opinion. First, say your opinion with I think or In my opinion. Next, give a reason with because. Then, add an example with For example. When you disagree, be kind and say, I see your point, but. When you don't understand, ask, What do you mean by this word? Listen to the end and don't interrupt. Remember, we can disagree and still be good friends." Which word do we use to give a reason? Because. What should we do when we disagree? Be kind. Birimo laughs. OK, no monkey. Maybe a hamster! Now, phonics. Spect means look, and re means again. When you respect someone, you look at them with kind eyes. Dict means say, and contra means against. To contradict means to say the opposite. Struct means build, and con means together. To construct means to build. Next time, we will explain a graph about plastic! | Hello, everyone! Now, phonics. Spect means look, and re means again. When you respect someone, you look at them with kind eyes. Dict means say, and contra means against. To contradict means to say the opposite. | 1045 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The graph shows that our class used less plastic this month. Soon, children from many places will come for the big council meeting. Olive says, We need forty folding chairs in a circle. Let's get ready together. Ms. Emma says, Let's review polite requests. Haru sees Leo and asks, Would you mind helping us set up the chairs? Say it with me. Would you mind helping us set up the chairs? Great! Leo says, Not at all! Remember, Not at all means, Sure, I'm happy to help. Then Riko asks Ms. Emma, Could you put a name card on each chair? Ms. Emma says, Sure, no problem. Let's read Olive's checklist. "Big Council Meeting Checklist. Put forty folding chairs in a big circle. Place a name card on each chair. Fill the water jugs and put them on the side table. Hang the welcome banner over the main door with an adult. Keep the aisles clear so that everyone can walk safely. Take a break when you feel tired, and drink some water. When everything is ready, we will meet at four o'clock by the Tree of Voices." How many chairs do we need? Forty. When will we meet? At four o'clock. Then Birimo climbs onto a chair to hang the banner. Ms. Emma says, Birimo, would you mind not standing on the chair? Let's ask an adult with a ladder. Birimo says, Oh, sorry! Good idea. Kiko says, I think this was our best teamwork, because everyone helped. Riko says, I agree. Suddenly, a fifth golden leaf grows on the Tree of Voices! Now, phonics. Let's review. Export means to carry things out to other countries. Ject means throw, so reject means to throw back, or to say no. Purchase is the formal word for buy. It ends in a, s, e. Should we meet at three? No, let's meet at FOUR. Next time, we will read two opinions about homework! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The graph shows that our class used less plastic this month. | 1046 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Would you mind helping us set up the chairs? The chairs are ready, and the fifth golden leaf is shining. Today, Olive gives us the Children's Council newsletter. Inside, there are two short opinion pieces about homework. One is by Leo, and one is by Priya. Ms. Emma says, Let's think like a judge and check the reasons. Read two opinions. Which do you agree with? Say it with me. Read two opinions. Which do you agree with? Nice! Let's read the two opinion pieces. "Leo writes, I think we should have homework every day. When we practice a little every day, we remember more. For example, I learned my times tables with ten minutes of homework a day. Priya writes, I think some days without homework are a good idea. Children need time to read, play and rest. For example, on free evenings, I read science books and go to bed early." What does Leo think? We should have homework every day. What does Priya do on free evenings? She reads science books and goes to bed early. Leo learned his times tables with ten minutes of homework a day. Is that a fact or an opinion? It's a fact. It really happened. Some days without homework are a good idea. That one is an opinion. Haru says, I agree with Leo because practice helps us remember. Riko says, I see your point, but rest is important, too. Kiko says, Both writers give good reasons. Maybe the best answer is somewhere in the middle, like short homework on most days and some free days. Now, phonics. Let's review. Popularity is popular plus ity. The internet has inter, which means between, because computers connect with each other. Do you feed the cat? No, I feed the DOG. Require is the formal word for need. Respect has spect, which means look. Next time, we will write a proposal letter to Mayor Hart! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Would you mind helping us set up the chairs? | 1048 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Dear Mayor, I suggest that we put more benches in the park. Today, we are practicing for the big council meeting, because there will be a friendly debate. The question is, Should every school have a school garden? In a debate, one team says yes, and the other team says no. Sometimes you speak for a side you don't agree with. That helps us understand others. Haru and Kiko are on the Yes team. Riko and Birimo are on the No team. Olive sets a timer for one minute. Haru stands up and begins. First, I'd like to say that school gardens teach us about nature. Say it with me. First, I'd like to say that school gardens teach us about nature. Excellent! Let's read the notes from both teams. "Yes Team. School gardens teach us where our food comes from. Children can grow vegetables and flowers, and the flowers help bees. No Team. Gardens need a lot of time and water. Someone has to take care of the plants in the summer holidays. Some schools are very small and have no space, so a garden might be difficult. A plant box in each classroom could be another idea." What do the flowers in a school garden help? Bees. What is one problem in the holidays? Someone has to take care of the plants. Riko says, That's true, but who will water the plants in the holidays? Kiko answers, I see your point. Maybe families can take turns. At the end, Ms. Emma says, To sum up, both teams made good points. Everyone shakes hands. Now, phonics. Let's review. Ability comes from able. Ble changes to bil. An intersection is where roads cross, and inter means between. Do you wash the dishes? No, I DRY the dishes. Inform is the formal word for tell. Construct has struct, which means build. Next time, we will look back at our days at the Hall! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Dear Mayor, I suggest that we put more benches in the park. | 1049 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, I'd like to say that school gardens teach us about nature. Tomorrow is the big council meeting, and then we will say goodbye to the Hall of Wisdom. Today, we sit under the Tree of Voices and look back at our time here. Kiko feels two things at once. She says, On the one hand, I'm sad to leave. On the other hand, I'm excited about our next adventure. Say it with me. On the one hand, I'm sad to leave. On the other hand, I'm excited about our next adventure. Beautiful! Let's read Olive's memory board. "Our Days at the Hall of Wisdom. We talked about school rules and listened to both sides. We learned to be fair, to stand up for a friend, and to invite everyone to play. We learned about children's rights and our responsibilities. We made a visitor map for Willowford, wrote a letter to Mayor Hart, and used less plastic. Most of all, we learned that good listeners make good speakers." What did we make for Willowford? A visitor map. What did we learn most of all? Good listeners make good speakers. Then Birimo goes to return a book to the Hall's library with Ms. Emma. On the top bookshelf, he sees a very old map. Ms. Emma reaches up and takes it down for him. The map shows a path that goes down, down, down to the bottom of the sea. At the end, there is a tiny picture of shining domes and the words Deep Sea City. Birimo whispers, Wow! Now, phonics. Let's review. Curiosity comes from curious. Ous changes to os, then add ity. Birimo is full of curiosity! A costar is a star in a movie together with another star. Do you clean on Sunday? No, we clean on SATURDAY. Provide is the formal word for give. Transport means to carry across. Next time, it's our grand finale! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, I'd like to say that school gardens teach us about nature. | 1049 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, I'd like to say that school gardens teach us about nature. She says, On the one hand, I'm sad to leave. On the other hand, I'm excited about our next adventure. Say it with me. On the one hand, I'm sad to leave. On the other hand, I'm excited about our next adventure. Beautiful! Let's read Olive's memory board. "Our Days at the Hall of Wisdom. We talked about school rules and listened to both sides. We learned to be fair, to stand up for a friend, and to invite everyone to play. We learned about children's rights and our responsibilities. We made a visitor map for Willowford, wrote a letter to Mayor Hart, and used less plastic. Most of all, we learned that good listeners make good speakers." What did we make for Willowford? | 1051 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Children's voices can change the world. Birimo's glowing map leads us, with Ms. Emma, to a small research harbor by the sea. There, we meet Dr. Marina Vale, a kind ocean scientist with a silver diving watch. She points to a little round submarine with thick round windows and bright lamps. This is the Bluebell, she says. I'm the pilot, so you're safe with me. Down we go! Through the porthole, the water turns from light blue to dark blue, and then to black. Dr. Vale says, Below about 1,000 meters, it is completely dark. Kiko whispers, This is the place where light never reaches. Say it with me. This is the place where light never reaches. Wonderful! Then soft blue lights appear below us. Let's read the welcome sign at the city gate. "Welcome to Deep Sea City! You are now 3,800 meters under the sea. Our city is made of glass domes, and tunnels join them together. Every dome is full of fresh air, so you can walk and breathe just like at home. Visit the gardens under our blue lamps, the Research Center, the Coral Library, and the aquarium hall with its big windows. In the Coral Library, you can find books about almost every creature in the sea. In the middle stands the Great Dome, the biggest dome of all. Please stay with your group and enjoy your visit. Our guides are always happy to help!" How deep is Deep Sea City? 3,800 meters. What is inside every dome? Fresh air. A little lanternfish with glowing blue spots swims up in a water tunnel next to us. Her voice comes from a shell speaker. Hi! I'm Lumi! She is carrying a glass lamp with six empty pearl holders. This is the Lamp of Knowledge, Lumi says. In one month, we will hold our Research Festival in the Great Dome, and young researchers will present what they found. When you learn a new research skill, a glowing pearl will appear in the lamp. Let's find out! Now, phonics. Bio means life. Biology is the study of living things. A biography is a book about someone's life. A biome is a large area with its own climate, plants and animals. The deep sea is a biome, too! Next time, we'll visit the aquarium hall and see creatures that glow. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Children's voices can change the world. | 1052 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This is the place where light never reaches. Today, Lumi takes us to the aquarium hall with Ms. Emma and Dr. Vale. The hall has huge windows that look out into the deep sea. Dr. Vale turns off the room lights, and we wait quietly. At first, everything is black. Then, little by little, we see tiny lights. Blue lights, green lights, and lights that blink on and off! Riko whispers, No way! Where is that light coming from? Dr. Vale smiles and says, The animals are making it. Many deep-sea animals make their own light. Scientists call this bioluminescence. Haru tries to say the long word, and everyone laughs. Then Kiko says it in a simple way. Some deep-sea fish make their own light. Say it with me. Some deep-sea fish make their own light. Excellent! Let's read the sign next to the big window. "Lights in the Dark. Down here, there is no sunlight, but you can still see tiny lights. Many deep-sea animals make their own light. Scientists call this bioluminescence. Why do they glow? Some use light to find food. Some use it to hide, and some use it to talk to each other. Others flash to scare away enemies. Look at the anglerfish in Window 3. Some anglerfish have a glowing lure on their head. The light attracts small fish, and then, gulp! Now look at Window 5. These small fish are lanternfish. Their glowing spots shine like tiny stars. There are huge numbers of lanternfish in the ocean. Please do not tap on the glass." Why do some animals glow? To find food, to hide, to talk to each other, or to scare away enemies. What does the anglerfish's lure do? It attracts small fish. Lumi swims next to the window and blinks her blue spots. Now you know my secret! I'm a lanternfish, too! Now, phonics. Bio means life. Bioluminescent means making your own light. It's bio plus luminescent, which means giving light. Lanternfish are bioluminescent. Biodiversity means the variety of living things. The deep sea has amazing biodiversity. The biosphere is all the parts of the Earth where life exists, from the highest mountains to the deepest sea. Next time, we'll find out why the deep sea is so dark. | Hello, everyone! Lanternfish are bioluminescent. Biodiversity means the variety of living things. The deep sea has amazing biodiversity. The biosphere is all the parts of the Earth where life exists, from the highest mountains to the deepest sea. | 1053 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Some deep-sea fish make their own light. Today, Dr. Vale takes us to the observation room in the Research Center. On the wall, there is a tall chart of the ocean, from the surface all the way down to the seafloor. At the top, it is bright blue. In the middle, it gets darker. At the bottom, it is black. Birimo looks out the window and asks, Why is it so dark out there? Did somebody turn off the sea? Everyone laughs. Haru guesses, Maybe the water is dirty? Dr. Vale shakes her head. Sunlight comes from above, and the water slowly takes it away. So the deeper you go, the less light there is. Then Haru says it clearly. The reason why it's dark is that sunlight can't reach this deep. Say it with me. The reason why it's dark is that sunlight can't reach this deep. Perfect! Let's read the depth chart. "How Deep Does Light Go? From the surface to about 200 meters: Sunlight reaches well only this top part of the ocean. This is the bright part, where the water looks blue. From 200 meters to 1,000 meters: The water gets darker and darker as you go down. Below about 1,000 meters: It is completely dark. Scientists call this the midnight zone, because it is like night all the time. The water down here is very cold, about 2 to 4 degrees. The deepest known place in the ocean is in the Mariana Trench, about 11,000 meters deep. Much of the deep sea has never been explored, so there is still a lot to find out." How far does sunlight reach well? About 200 meters. How cold is the deep water? About 2 to 4 degrees. Kiko points to the bottom of the chart. And we are here, at 3,800 meters! Lumi says, That's why my blue spots are so useful! Now, phonics. Bio means life. A biographer is a person who writes biographies, books about someone's life. An antibiotic is a medicine that kills bacteria. Anti means against. Biodegradable means it can be broken down by tiny living things called bacteria. Banana peels are biodegradable. Next time, we'll learn about the strong push of the deep water. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Some deep-sea fish make their own light. | 1054 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, The reason why it's dark is that sunlight can't reach this deep. Today, Dr. Vale shows us something we cannot see: pressure. She asks us to put our hands together and push. Push harder! Harder! That's pressure. Water is heavy, and the deeper you go, the more water there is above you. So the water pushes harder and harder. Riko looks a little worried. Is the Bluebell okay? Dr. Vale laughs kindly. Don't worry. The Bluebell and the domes of Deep Sea City are built to be very, very strong. Then Kiko asks, But what about the fish outside? They don't have strong walls! Dr. Vale opens a big research card and says, That's the amazing part. Deep-sea animals have special bodies. Say it with me. Deep-sea animals have special bodies. Great job! Let's read the research card. "Life Under Pressure. As you go deeper, the water pushes harder. Every 10 meters adds about the same push as all the air above us. At 4,000 meters, the water pushes about 400 times harder than at the surface. So how do deep-sea animals live here? First, many of them have soft, jelly-like bodies. Second, many of them have no air sacs inside. Air can be squeezed, so without air sacs, the pressure doesn't crush them. Some animals also have very big eyes to catch tiny bits of light. And look at the dumbo octopus! Its fins look like big ears. Deep-sea bodies are just right for deep-sea life." How much harder does the water push at 4,000 meters? About 400 times harder than at the surface. Why do some animals have very big eyes? To catch tiny bits of light. Birimo flaps his hands next to his head and says, Look! I'm a dumbo octopus! Lumi giggles in her water tunnel. Now, phonics. Bio means life. Symbiosis is when two living things help each other. Sym means together. Biomimicry means copying nature's ideas to make inventions. Mimic means copy. Maybe someday, people will design new submarines by copying deep-sea animals! Biofuel is fuel made from plants. Next time, it's our Deep Sea Wonder Talk, and each of you will share one amazing fact. | Hello, everyone! | 1055 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Deep-sea animals have special bodies. Today is our first big day in Deep Sea City: the Deep Sea Wonder Talk! We gather in the garden dome under the blue lamps. Ms. Emma, Dr. Vale and Lumi sit in the front row. Each friend will share one amazing fact. Lumi has one important rule. When someone shares a fact, ask, Where did you find that? Haru goes first. He holds up his hands in two big circles. One amazing fact is that giant squid have eyes as big as plates. Say it with me. One amazing fact is that giant squid have eyes as big as plates. Fantastic! Let's read our Wonder Talk cards. "Deep Sea Wonder Talk Cards. Haru: Giant squid have eyes as big as dinner plates, about 27 centimeters across. Riko: Some anglerfish have a glowing lure on their head. The light attracts small fish. Kiko: Tube worms live near hydrothermal vents, which are hot springs on the seafloor. The water there can be hotter than 350 degrees, and the worms live there without any sunlight! Birimo: Vampire squid have a scary name, but they don't drink blood. They eat marine snow, which is made of tiny bits that fall slowly from above like snow. Listeners, remember to ask each speaker, Where did you find that?" How big are a giant squid's eyes? About 27 centimeters, as big as dinner plates. What do vampire squid eat? Marine snow. Lumi asks each friend, Where did you find that? Haru says, I read it on a sign in the aquarium hall. Riko says, Dr. Vale told me. Kiko says, I saw it on a poster in the Research Center. Birimo says, I read it on a sign in the garden dome. Lumi claps her fins. Wonderful! You all know where your facts came from. Then something magical happens. The Lamp of Knowledge begins to shine, and a glowing pearl appears in the first holder! Our first pearl! Now, phonics. Let's review. Biology is the study of living things. Bioluminescent means making your own light, just like Lumi. Symbiosis is when two living things help each other. Next time, we'll visit the Research Center and choose our very own research questions. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Deep-sea animals have special bodies. | 1057 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My research question is: How do deep-sea fish find food? Now we all have our research questions. But where can we find information? Dr. Vale says, Let's ask the best helper in Deep Sea City. Lumi leads us, with Ms. Emma, through a long glass tunnel to the Coral Library. The shelves are made of pink and orange coral, and the books glow softly under blue lamps. Behind the desk sits Mr. Oswin, a very old sea turtle with tiny reading glasses. He speaks very slowly. Wel... come... young... researchers. Haru asks, Mr. Oswin, where can I find information about giant squid? Mr. Oswin smiles and points to a poster with his flipper. Riko reads the poster and says, We can use books, websites and interviews. Say it with me. We can use books, websites and interviews. Well done! Let's read Mr. Oswin's poster. "Three Ways to Find Information. Books: Books in the Coral Library are written and checked carefully before they are printed. Use the table of contents and the index to find your topic quickly. Websites: Websites are fast, and many have pictures and videos. But anyone can make a website, so always ask who wrote it. Search online with a grown-up. Interviews: You can talk to a scientist or someone who knows a lot about your topic. Write your questions before the interview, listen carefully, and say thank you at the end. A good researcher uses more than one kind of source. And remember the most important rule in this library: Check your sources!" Why should we ask who wrote a website? Because anyone can make a website. What should we do before an interview? Write our questions. Mr. Oswin taps the last line of the poster and says his favorite words. Check... your... sources! Birimo tries to say it as slowly as Mr. Oswin, and everyone giggles. Now, phonics. Remember, long study words have one strong part. Evidence. EV-i-dence. Discover. Dis-COV-er. Explain. Ex-PLAIN. Information. In-for-MA-tion. Words that end in -tion are strong just before -tion. Information! Next time, we'll learn how to tell people where we found our information. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, My research question is: How do deep-sea fish find food? Now we all have our research questions. But where can we find information? Dr. Vale says, Let's ask the best helper in Deep Sea City. Lumi leads us through a long glass tunnel to the Coral Library. The shelves are made of pink and orange coral, and the books glow softly under blue lamps. Behind the desk sits Mr. Oswin, a very old sea turtle with tiny reading glasses. He speaks very slowly. Wel... come... young... researchers. Haru asks, Mr. Oswin, where can I find information about giant squid? Mr. Oswin smiles and points to a poster with his flipper. Riko reads the poster and says, We can use books, websites and interviews. Say it with me. We can use books, websites and interviews. Well done! Let's read Mr. Oswin's poster. "Three Ways to Find Information. Books: Books in the Coral Library are written and checked carefully before they are printed. Use the table of contents and the index to find your topic quickly. Websites: Websites are fast, and many have pictures and videos. But anyone can make a website, so always ask who wrote it. Search online with a grown-up. Interviews: You can talk to a scientist or someone who knows a lot about your topic. Write your questions before the interview, listen carefully, and say thank you at the end. A good researcher uses more than one kind of source. And remember the most important rule in this library: Check your sources!" Why should we ask who wrote a website? Because anyone can make a website. What should we do before an interview? Write our questions. Mr. Oswin taps the last line of the poster and says his favorite words. Check... your... sources! | 1059 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, According to this book, lanternfish swim up at night to eat. Today, Ms. Emma and Dr. Vale help us look for information online in the Research Center. Birimo is searching for anglerfish. Suddenly, he jumps up. Everyone, look! This website says anglerfish are as big as buses! Haru's mouth falls open. As big as buses? Riko frowns. Hmm. That sounds strange. Ms. Emma says, Good researchers ask questions about every website. Is it reliable（しんらい できる）? Reliable means we can trust it. To find out, we ask four questions. Who wrote it? When was it written? Does it show its sources? Can we find the same information in a book? Kiko says the first two questions out loud. Who wrote it? When was it written? Say it with me. Who wrote it? When was it written? Super! Let's read the web page Birimo found. "Fishy Fun Facts Page! Welcome to the coolest fish page in the whole world! Did you know? Anglerfish are as big as buses! Their lights are brighter than the sun! Giant squid can fly over the waves like birds! Lanternfish sing songs every night! Top Fact of the Week: Octopuses can drive tiny cars on the seafloor! All of these facts are one hundred percent true, we promise! We know everything about the ocean, so you never need to read a book again! Click here for more crazy facts! Click here to win a free submarine! Author: not shown. Date: not shown. Sources: none. Share this page with all your friends!" What does the web page say about anglerfish? They are as big as buses. Who wrote it? We don't know. There is no author and no date. Ms. Emma adds, And never click on prizes like that free submarine. Then Lumi brings a message from Mr. Oswin. He looked in all of his books, and none of them say that anglerfish are as big as buses. Birimo sighs. I almost believed it! Lumi says, It's okay, Birimo. Now you know how to check. Let's find out the real facts! Now, phonics. Here are four more study words. Presentation. Pres-en-TA-tion. It ends in -tion, so the strong part is just before it. Important. Im-POR-tant. Topic. TOP-ic. Conclude. Con-CLUDE. Next time, we'll put everything together and make our research plan. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, According to this book, lanternfish swim up at night to eat. Today, Dr. Vale and I help everyone look for information online in the Research Center. Birimo is searching for anglerfish. Suddenly, he jumps up. Everyone, look! This website says anglerfish are as big as buses! Haru's mouth falls open. As big as buses? Riko frowns. Hmm. That sounds strange. I say, Good researchers ask questions about every website. Is it reliable（しんらい できる）? Reliable means we can trust it. To find out, we ask four questions. Who wrote it? When was it written? Does it show its sources? Can we find the same information in a book? Kiko says the first two questions out loud. Who wrote it? When was it written? Say it with me. Who wrote it? When was it written? Super! Let's read the web page Birimo found. "Fishy Fun Facts Page! Welcome to the coolest fish page in the whole world! Did you know? Anglerfish are as big as buses! Their lights are brighter than the sun! Giant squid can fly over the waves like birds! Lanternfish sing songs every night! Top Fact of the Week: Octopuses can drive tiny cars on the seafloor! All of these facts are one hundred percent true, we promise! We know everything about the ocean, so you never need to read a book again! Click here for more crazy facts! Click here to win a free submarine! Author: not shown. Date: not shown. Sources: none. Share this page with all your friends!" What does the web page say about anglerfish? They are as big as buses. Who wrote it? We don't know. There is no author and no date. I add, And never click on prizes like that free submarine. Then Lumi brings a message from Mr. Oswin. He looked in all of his books, and none of them say that anglerfish are as big as buses. Birimo sighs. I almost believed it! Lumi says, It's OK, Birimo. Now you know how to check. Let's find out the real facts! Now, phonics. Here are four more study words. Presentation. Pres-en-TA-tion. It ends in -tion, so the strong part is just before it. Important. Im-POR-tant. Topic. TOP-ic. Conclude. Con-CLUDE. Next time, we'll put everything together and make our research plan. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, According to this book, lanternfish swim up at night to eat. | 1060 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Who wrote it? When was it written? Now we know how to choose a research question, where to find information, how to tell people our sources, and how to check a website. Dr. Vale says, The Research Festival is in about three weeks. A good researcher makes a plan before starting. So we sit around a big round table in the Research Center with Ms. Emma and make a research plan together. Kiko writes the steps on a big card, and Riko draws a little calendar. Birimo wants to interview a giant squid, but Haru says, Maybe we should start with a scientist. Dr. Vale smiles. I'd be happy to help. Haru reads the first two steps. First, we'll read books. Then, we'll interview a scientist. Say it with me. First, we'll read books. Then, we'll interview a scientist. Wonderful! Let's read our research plan card. "Our Research Plan. Team: Haru, Riko, Kiko and Birimo. Goal: Share what we found at the Research Festival in the Great Dome. Step 1: First, we'll read books in the Coral Library. Mr. Oswin will help us find them. Step 2: Then, we'll interview a scientist. Dr. Vale said yes! We will write our questions before we meet her and say thank you at the end. Step 3: Next, we'll check one or two websites with Ms. Emma. We will ask who wrote them and when. Step 4: Finally, we'll compare our sources and write down our results. Schedule: about three weeks. Remember: Check your sources!" Who will help us find books? Mr. Oswin. What will we do in Step 4? We'll compare our sources and write down our results. We read our plan to Lumi. She swims in a happy circle and says, Let's find out! Then the Lamp of Knowledge begins to glow, and a second pearl appears next to the first one. Two pearls! Now, phonics. Let's review. Research. Re-SEARCH. It's what we're doing right now! Result. Re-SULT. A result is what we find at the end. And a biography is a book about someone's life. Bio means life, and graphy means writing. Next time, we'll learn how to say a long explanation in a short way. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Who wrote it? When was it written? | 1061 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, we'll read books. Then, we'll interview a scientist. Ms. Emma and Dr. Vale take us to the Coral Library. Mr. Oswin, the old sea turtle, slowly pushes a thick book across the table. He looks at us over his tiny reading glasses and says, This book comes from the Research Center. Always check your sources, and this one is a good one! Riko opens it to a long page about anglerfish, because her research question is, How do deep-sea fish find food? It takes her five minutes! Then Lumi swims up to the glass of her water tunnel and says through the shell speaker, Riko, that was a lot of information. Can you say it in one sentence? Riko thinks. Then she says, In short, anglerfish use light to catch food. In short（つまり） means we keep only the most important idea and cut the extra details. Say it with me. In short, anglerfish use light to catch food. Great! Let's read the page from Mr. Oswin's book. "How Anglerfish Catch Their Food. Anglerfish live in the deep sea, where it is completely dark. Food is hard to find down there, so many anglerfish don't swim around looking for it. Instead, they wait. Some anglerfish have a glowing lure on their head, which looks a little like a fishing rod with a light at the end. The anglerfish stays very still and moves the lure slowly. Small fish see the light and swim closer, because they think it might be something to eat. Then, very quickly, the anglerfish opens its big mouth and gulps them down. Scientists are still finding out more about these amazing fish." Where do anglerfish live? In the deep sea, where it is completely dark. Why do small fish swim closer? Because they think the light might be something to eat. Haru laughs and says, In short, anglerfish are very patient! Now, phonics. When we give a talk, we pause in the right places. In short, pause, the deep sea is dark and cold. Anglerfish live in the dark, pause, so they make their own light. The animals that live down there, pause, have special bodies. As you know, pause, sunlight cannot reach the bottom. But never pause in the middle of a group like the deep sea! Next time, we'll find the big idea of a whole article. | Hello, everyone! | 1062 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In short, anglerfish use light to catch food. Today, Dr. Vale invites us to the Research Center. She has written a short article for the city newspaper, and she wants young readers to check it before she shares it. Kiko reads it first. She says, There are so many facts! Which one is the most important? Dr. Vale taps her silver diving watch and smiles. Good researchers ask, What is the writer really trying to tell us? That's the main point. The other facts are details. Details support the main point, but they're not the big idea. Kiko reads the article again, more slowly this time. Then she says, The main point is that the ocean needs protecting. Say it with me. The main point is that the ocean needs protecting. Excellent! Let's read Dr. Vale's article. "The Deep Sea Needs Our Help, by Dr. Marina Vale. Much of the deep sea has never been explored. Down in the dark live amazing animals, like octopuses with fins that look like big ears and fish that make their own light. But the deep sea is not as far away from us as you might think. Scientists have found plastic even in the deepest trench, about 11,000 meters down. Things we throw away on land can end up in the ocean. If we want to keep learning about these animals, we need to take care of their home. We can use less plastic, and we can share what we learn with our families. The ocean needs protecting, and young researchers can help." What have scientists found in the deepest trench? Plastic. What can we do to help? We can use less plastic and share what we learn. Haru asks, Is the octopus with big ear fins a main point? Dr. Vale shakes her head. It's a fun detail, but it's a detail. Birimo wiggles happily and says, Well, it's my favorite detail! Everyone laughs. Now, phonics. Let's pause in the right places again. The main point is, pause, that the ocean needs our help. According to this book, pause, giant squid have huge eyes. Most of the deep sea, pause, has never been explored. Like a flashlight, pause, some fish can make light. A pause gives your listeners time to think. Next time, we'll read about something strange that falls like snow in the deep sea! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In short, anglerfish use light to catch food. Today, Dr. Vale invites us to the Research Center. She has written a short article for the city newspaper, and she wants young readers to check it before she shares it. Kiko reads it first. That's the main point. The other facts are details. Details support the main point, but they're not the big idea. Kiko reads the article again, more slowly this time. Then she says, The main point is that the ocean needs protecting. Say it with me. The main point is that the ocean needs protecting. Excellent! Let's read Dr. Vale's article. "The Deep Sea Needs Our Help, by Dr. Marina Vale. Much of the deep sea has never been explored. Down in the dark live amazing animals, like octopuses with fins that look like big ears and fish that make their own light. But the deep sea is not as far away from us as you might think. Scientists have found plastic even in the deepest trench, about 11,000 meters down. Things we throw away on land can end up in the ocean. If we want to keep learning about these animals, we need to take care of their home. We can use less plastic, and we can share what we learn with our families. The ocean needs protecting, and young researchers can help." What have scientists found in the deepest trench? Plastic. What can we do to help? We can use less plastic and share what we learn. Haru asks, Is the octopus with big ear fins a main point? Dr. Vale shakes her head. It's a fun fact, but it's a detail. Birimo wiggles happily and says, Well, it's my favorite detail! | 1064 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, This article is about marine snow. It says that tiny bits of food fall like snow. It shows how deep-sea animals get food. Today, Dr. Vale gives a short talk at the Research Center, and Kiko is very excited, because the talk is about her research question: Why do some animals glow? Kiko opens her notebook and starts writing every single word. Dr. Vale talks, and Kiko writes, writes, writes. Oh no! Dr. Vale is already three sentences ahead! Ms. Emma whispers, Kiko, you don't need to write every word. Just write the keywords. Keywords are the most important words. Kiko tries again, and now her notes look like this. Take notes: glow, food, dark. Say it with me. Take notes: glow, food, dark. Super! Short words are quick to write, and later, they help you remember the whole idea. You can draw arrows between your keywords, too, and color the most important one with a highlighter. Let's read part of Dr. Vale's talk. "Why do some animals glow? Many deep-sea animals make their own light. We call this bioluminescence. But why do they do it? Scientists have found a few reasons. Some animals glow to find food. For example, some anglerfish use a glowing lure to attract small fish. Some animals use light to hide from their enemies. Some use light to talk to each other, a bit like flashing signals in the dark. And some make a sudden bright flash to scare enemies away. Remember, it is completely dark down here, so light is a very useful tool. And there may be more reasons that we haven't discovered yet." What are the four reasons? To find food, to hide, to talk to each other, and to scare enemies away. Which word did Kiko color with her highlighter? Glow, of course! Now, phonics. Let's practice pausing one more time. If you take notes, pause, you can remember more. Write short words, pause, not long sentences. The most important word in this paragraph, pause, is glow. After you read, pause, look at your notes again. Kiko holds up her notes and says, I can remember the whole talk now! | 1065 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Take notes: glow, food, dark. Today, Lumi has a surprise for us in the aquarium hall. She swims in happy circles in her water tunnel, and her blue spots sparkle. Ms. Emma puts a big sand timer on the table. Lumi says, It's time for the Summary Challenge! Each of you has thirty seconds to tell us what you learned. Ready? Can you summarize it in 30 seconds? Say it with me. Can you summarize it in 30 seconds? Great! Let's read Lumi's challenge card. "The Summary Challenge. Step one: Look at your notes, not at a long page. Step two: When it's your turn, turn over the sand timer. Step three: Start with your topic, and then give the main point. You can use In short or The main point is. Step four: Give one or two details, not ten. Step five: Stop when the sand runs out. Everyone listens and cheers, and nobody laughs at mistakes. Remember, a good summary is short, clear and true. If you're not sure about a fact, say so. Don't forget to say where you found your facts. Breathe and smile before you start. Good luck, young researchers!" How long does each friend have? Thirty seconds. What should a good summary be? Short, clear and true. Riko goes first. In short, anglerfish use light to catch food. Then Kiko talks about the four reasons why animals glow. Haru says, The main point is that giant squid are really big. He even remembers his source: the biggest ones measured are about 12 to 13 meters long. Birimo goes last. He says, Scientists are still finding out if deep-sea animals sleep. In short, it's a mystery! The sand runs out just as he finishes. Everybody cheers! Then the Lamp of Knowledge begins to glow, and a third pearl appears inside it. Lumi says, You learned how to summarize! Now, phonics. Let's review. In short, pause, the deep sea is dark and cold. Experiment has a strong PER: ex-PER-i-ment. Biodiversity begins with bio, which means life. It means lots of different kinds of living things. Next time, we'll start learning how to give a real presentation! | Hello, everyone! | 1066 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Can you summarize it in 30 seconds? Today, Lumi takes us to the Great Dome for the first time. It's huge! There are rows and rows of seats, a round stage, and a big screen. Lumi says, This is where the Research Festival will be. Young researchers from all over the city will stand right here At the festival, each of you will give a research talk about your own research question. Haru gulps. Here? In front of all those seats? Dr. Vale laughs kindly. Don't worry, Haru. Every good talk starts with a good opening, and an opening is easy to learn. First, say hello. Next, ask a fun question to wake up your listeners. Then, tell them your topic. Riko walks to the middle of the stage and tries it. Hello, everyone. Did you know that some fish make their own light? Today, I'd like to talk about the deep sea. Say it with me. Today, I'd like to talk about the deep sea. Great! Let's read the tips Dr. Vale wrote on the board. "How to Start a Great Talk. One: Walk to the middle of the stage, stand up straight, and smile. Two: Say hello to your listeners. Good morning, everyone! is a friendly way to start. Three: Ask a question or share a surprising fact. For example, ask, Have you ever seen a fish that glows? Or share a fact, like this one: below about 1,000 meters, the sea is completely dark. This makes your listeners curious. Four: Tell them your topic. Start with, Today, I'd like to talk about... Five: Breathe! The first ten seconds are the hardest. After that, it gets easier. Your listeners want you to do well!" What should you do after you say hello? Ask a question or share a surprising fact. Why? Because it makes your listeners curious. Birimo jumps onto the stage. Good morning, fish and friends! Then Haru tries. His voice shakes a little, but he finishes, and everyone claps. Now, phonics. Ology means the study of. Biology is the study of living things, and a biologist is a person who studies them. Dr. Vale is a marine biologist! Zoology is the study of animals, and a zoologist studies animals. Say the strong part: bi-OL-o-gist, zo-OL-o-gy, zo-OL-o-gist. Next time, we'll put our points in order with first, second and finally! | Hello, everyone! | 1067 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Today, I'd like to talk about the deep sea. At the festival, the four friends will begin with a short team talk called Three Things About the Deep Sea, and Haru is writing it. He has a great opening now, but the rest of his talk is a mess. He has twenty facts on one long page, and they're all mixed up! Back at the Research Center, Dr. Vale hands him a small stack of note cards. She says, Write one point on each card. Then put the cards in order. Three points are usually enough for a short talk. Haru picks his three best points and lines up the cards on the table. Then he practices with three helpful words: first, second and finally. First, the deep sea is dark. Second, it is cold. Finally, the pressure is strong. Say it with me. First, the deep sea is dark. Second, it is cold. Finally, the pressure is strong. Fantastic! These words are like signs on a path. They tell your listeners where they are in your talk. Let's read Haru's note cards. "Card one. First, the deep sea is dark. Sunlight reaches well only the top part of the ocean, about 200 meters. Below about 1,000 meters, it is completely dark. Card two. Second, it is cold. Deep water is only about 2 to 4 degrees. Brr! Card three. Finally, the pressure is strong. Every 10 meters down adds about the same push as all the air above us. At 4,000 meters, the water pushes about 400 times harder than at the surface. Many deep-sea animals have soft, jelly-like bodies, so the pressure doesn't crush them. That's also why the Bluebell has such thick walls and windows." How cold is deep water? About 2 to 4 degrees. How much harder does the water push at 4,000 meters? About 400 times harder. Kiko says, Your talk is so easy to follow now! Haru grins. And my giant squid facts can go in my own talk! Now, phonics. Ecology is the study of how living things and their homes are connected. An ecologist studies ecology. Technology means the tools and machines that people invent, like the Bluebell. Remember the strong part: e-COL-o-gy, e-COL-o-gist, tech-NOL-o-gy. | 1068 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, the deep sea is dark. Second, it is cold. Finally, the pressure is strong. Today, Riko is getting her talk ready in the Great Dome. Dr. Vale shows her how to use the big screen. Riko can put pictures and a graph on her slides and point to them with a long glowing pointer. Dr. Vale says, Pictures help your listeners understand quickly. But don't just show a picture. Tell your listeners what to look at. Riko clicks, and a picture of an anglerfish fills the screen. She points to the little light on its head and says, As you can see in this picture, the anglerfish has a glowing light. Say it with me. As you can see in this picture, the anglerfish has a glowing light. Excellent! Next, Riko shows a graph about depth and light. Let's read the words for her slide. "Slide Three: Light in the Ocean. This graph shows how much sunlight there is at different depths. At the top, near the surface, the water is bright and blue. But sunlight reaches well only the top part of the ocean, about 200 meters. After that, the line goes down quickly. Below about 1,000 meters, the line reaches zero. There is no sunlight at all. This is called the midnight zone. Deep Sea City is at 3,800 meters, so it is completely dark outside the domes. In a place with no sunlight, light from animals is very important. That's why the anglerfish uses its glowing lure to find food." How deep does sunlight reach well? About 200 meters. What happens below about 1,000 meters? It is completely dark. Then Birimo, sitting in the back row, waves his arms. Riko, I can't see the bottom of the graph! Riko makes the graph bigger, and she steps to the side so she isn't standing in front of the screen. Much better! Now, phonics. Archaeology is the study of people from long ago. We learn about them from the old things they left behind. An archaeologist digs up old pots and tools. Mythology means the old stories about gods and heroes. Say the strong part: ar-chae-OL-o-gy, ar-chae-OL-o-gist, my-THOL-o-gy. Next time, we'll learn how to finish a talk with a strong ending! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, First, the deep sea is dark. Second, it is cold. Finally, the pressure is strong. | 1069 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, As you can see in this picture, the anglerfish has a glowing light. Today, Kiko practices her whole talk for Lumi in front of the big windows of the aquarium hall. Her opening is great, and her three points are clear. But at the very end, she just says, That's all. Bye! and runs off the stage. Lumi blinks her blue spots. Kiko, that was a bit sudden! A good talk needs a good ending, too. Ms. Emma explains that the ending of a talk is called the conclusion. In the conclusion, we say our main point again, so our listeners remember it. Then we can add a short message or a hope. We often start with two special words: in conclusion. Kiko thinks about her whole talk. What does she want everyone to remember? Then she says, In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Say it with me. In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Perfect! Let's read the new ending of Kiko's talk. "So why do some animals glow? Many deep-sea animals make their own light, and they use it in clever ways. Some glow to find food, some glow to hide, some use light to talk to each other, and some flash to scare enemies away. Before I started my research, I thought the deep sea was just dark and empty. Now I know that it is full of tiny lights, like stars in the night sky. Much of the deep sea has never been explored, so there may be glowing animals that nobody has seen yet. I hope you will keep asking questions about the ocean, too. In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Thank you!" What does Kiko say again in her ending? The four reasons why animals glow. What did Kiko think before her research? She thought the deep sea was just dark and empty. Lumi spins happily in her tunnel. That ending gave me goosebumps, and I'm a fish! Now, phonics. Meteorology is the study of weather. A meteorologist studies the weather and tells us if it will rain tomorrow. A volcanologist studies volcanoes, like Dr. Mori on the Island of Fire. Remember the strong part: me-te-or-OL-o-gy, me-te-or-OL-o-gist, vol-can-OL-o-gist. Next time, we'll have a big practice day in the Great Dome! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, As you can see in this picture, the anglerfish has a glowing light. Today, Kiko practices her whole talk for Lumi in front of the big windows of the aquarium hall. Then she says, In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Say it with me. In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Perfect! Let's read the new ending of Kiko's talk. "So why do some animals glow? Many deep-sea animals make their own light, and they use it in clever ways. Now I know that it is full of tiny lights, like stars in the night sky. Much of the deep sea has never been explored, so there may be glowing animals that nobody has seen yet. I hope you will keep asking questions about the ocean, too. In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Thank you!" What does Kiko say again in her ending? The four reasons why animals glow. | 1070 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In conclusion, the deep sea is full of surprises. Today is rehearsal day in the Great Dome. Lumi, Dr. Vale and even Mr. Oswin are sitting in the front row. Mr. Oswin came very slowly, of course, but he's here! Haru goes first with our team talk. He's nervous, so he talks very, very fast. Firstthedeepseaisdarksecondit... Nobody can follow him! Mr. Oswin raises one flipper and says, Slow down, young one. Even a turtle can win a race if he takes his time. Ms. Emma gives Haru two tips. Speak slowly. Pause after each point. Say it with me. Speak slowly. Pause after each point. Well done! Pauses give your listeners time to think, and they give you time to breathe. Let's read the tips Dr. Vale wrote on our rehearsal card. "Rehearsal Tips for Young Researchers. Speak slowly. It may feel too slow to you, but it's just right for your audience. Pause after each point, and count one, two in your head. Look at the audience, not at your notes. Make eye contact with people on the left, in the middle and on the right. Smile! When you smile, your audience smiles too. Speak loudly enough for the people in the back row to hear you. If you make a mistake, that's OK. Just take a breath and keep going. Practice your talk three times with a friend or a family member, and you'll feel much more confident." What should you do after each point? Pause and count one, two in your head. Who should you make eye contact with? People on the left, in the middle and on the right. Haru tries again. This time, he speaks slowly, pauses after each point, looks around and smiles. Then Riko, Kiko and Birimo practice their own talks, too. When the four friends bow together, the Lamp of Knowledge glows, and a fourth pearl appears! Lumi cheers, You learned how to present! Now, phonics. Let's review. A biologist studies living things: bi-OL-o-gist. Most of the deep sea, pause, has never been explored. Discover has a strong COV: dis-COV-er. And the deep sea is a biome, a big area with its own climate, plants and animals. Next time, we'll learn how to answer questions from the audience! | Hello, everyone! | 1071 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Speak slowly. Pause after each point. Today, we are back in the Research Center. Kiko gives her practice talk about why some animals glow. She speaks slowly and clearly, and everyone claps. Then Dr. Vale says, Now it's question time! Birimo raises his hand right away. Kiko, why is it so dark down here? Kiko's cheeks turn pink. She feels a little nervous. But Lumi swims up to the window and whispers through the shell speaker, Take a breath. You know this! When someone asks you a question, you can say something kind first. That gives you a moment to think. Then you can give your answer. That's a great question. Let me explain. Say it with me. That's a great question. Let me explain. Great! Let's read the card that Dr. Vale puts on the table. "How to Answer a Question. Listen to the whole question, and don't start talking too early. Look at the person who asked it, and smile. Say something kind, like That's a great question. Then say, Let me explain. Give a short answer first, and then add one reason or one example. For example, if someone asks why the deep sea is dark, you can say that sunlight only reaches the top 200 meters or so. Below about 1,000 meters, it is completely dark. At the end, ask, Does that answer your question? Remember, a question is a gift. It shows that people were really listening to you." What should you do before you answer? Listen to the whole question. How deep does sunlight reach well? About 200 meters. Kiko tries again. That's a great question. Let me explain. Sunlight only reaches about 200 meters, and Deep Sea City is at 3,800 meters. Birimo nods happily. Does that answer your question? Yes, thank you! Now, phonics. Let's read big numbers with units. 3,800 meters is three thousand eight hundred meters. 11,000 meters is eleven thousand meters. That's about how deep the deepest known place is, in the Mariana Trench. 200 meters is two hundred meters. After numbers bigger than one, we say meters with an s. Next time, Riko gets a question she can't answer. What will she do? | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Speak slowly. Pause after each point. Today, we are back in the Research Center.
