| 1072 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, That's a great question. Let me explain. Today, it's Riko's turn. She practices her talk about how deep-sea fish find food. She talks about big eyes, glowing lights and marine snow. Then Haru raises his hand. Riko, how cold is the water outside the dome? Riko stops. She didn't write that in her notes. What can she do? She doesn't want to make up a number. Lumi says, When you don't know for sure, be honest! Tell people what you think, and tell them you're not sure. So Riko takes a breath and makes a careful guess. I'm not sure, but I think the water is about four degrees. Say it with me. I'm not sure, but I think the water is about four degrees. Great! Then Riko asks Dr. Vale to check. Dr. Vale smiles and shows us a page from her science notebook. Let's read it. "Water Temperature in the Ocean. Near the surface, the water can be warm, especially in sunny places. But the deeper you go, the colder it gets. Deep water is very cold, about 2 to 4 degrees, even in hot parts of the world. Near the North and South Poles, sea water can be as cold as minus 2 degrees. But there are surprises on the seafloor, too. In some places, there are hot springs called hydrothermal vents. The water that comes out of them can be hotter than 350 degrees! Tube worms live near these vents, and they don't need any sunlight. The ocean is full of surprises, so a good researcher always checks the facts." How cold is deep water? About 2 to 4 degrees. What lives near hydrothermal vents? Tube worms. So Riko's guess was right! Dr. Vale says, Good job, Riko. You were honest, and you made a smart guess. Then you checked it. That's what real scientists do. Now, phonics. Let's read temperatures. −2°C is minus two degrees. 4°C is four degrees. 350°C is three hundred fifty degrees. We say one degree, but two degrees, with an s. Next time, we'll learn how to ask good questions, too! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, That's a great question. Let me explain. | 1073 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I'm not sure, but I think the water is about four degrees. Today, Haru gives his practice talk. His research question is, How big can giant squid grow? He shows a picture of a giant squid with its long arms and tentacles. He speaks slowly and pauses after each point. Everyone listens carefully. When Haru finishes, Ms. Emma says, Good listeners ask good questions. A good question starts with the topic, so the speaker knows what you want to ask about. Kiko raises her hand. I have a question about the giant squid. Say it with me. I have a question about the giant squid. Great! Let's read the question guide on the wall of the Research Center. "How to Ask a Good Question. First, listen to the whole talk. Write down one or two things you want to know more about. Wait until the speaker asks for questions, and then raise your hand. Start with the topic. For example, say, I have a question about the giant squid. Then ask one clear question, not three questions at once. Good questions often start with how, why, which or where. After the answer, say thank you. If you want to know more, you can ask a follow-up question. Remember, a good question helps everyone in the room learn something new, and that includes the speaker!" When should you raise your hand? When the speaker asks for questions. What words do good questions often start with? How, why, which or where. Kiko asks, How big are its eyes? Haru answers, They're as big as dinner plates, about 27 centimeters. Then Riko asks a follow-up question. Which are bigger, males or females? Haru says, Females are bigger. The biggest ones that scientists have measured are about 12 to 13 meters long. Now, phonics. Let's read lengths. 13 meters is thirteen meters. 27 cm is twenty-seven centimeters. 11 km is eleven kilometers. That's about how deep the Mariana Trench is! Next time, Birimo gets a question, and he has a clever plan. | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I'm not sure, but I think the water is about four degrees. Today, Haru gives his practice talk. | 1074 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, I have a question about the giant squid. Today, it's Birimo's turn. His research question is, Do deep-sea animals sleep? He explains that some fish seem to rest quietly, but scientists are still finding out. After his talk, Haru raises his hand. I have a question about lanternfish. Do lanternfish sleep, too? Birimo thinks and thinks. Hmm. I don't know yet. He doesn't want to guess this time. So he says, I'll look it up and tell you later. Say it with me. I'll look it up and tell you later. Great! It's a promise, so Birimo really has to do it. After lunch, he goes to the Coral Library with Ms. Emma. Mr. Oswin, the old sea turtle, pushes up his tiny reading glasses. Then he opens a big encyclopedia, very, very slowly. Check your sources! he says. Let's read the page. "Lanternfish. Lanternfish are small fish with glowing spots on their bodies. There are huge numbers of them in the ocean. In the daytime, many lanternfish stay deep down, where it is dark. At night, many of them swim up toward the surface to eat, and then they go back down again. Do lanternfish sleep? Scientists are still finding out. It is not easy to watch fish in the deep sea, because it is dark and very far away. Much of the deep sea has never been explored, so there are still many questions for young researchers to answer. Maybe you will be one of them!" What do many lanternfish do at night? They swim up toward the surface to eat. Do lanternfish sleep? Scientists are still finding out. The next morning, Birimo runs to Haru. I looked it up! Haru says, Really? What did you find out? Birimo tells him everything. He kept his promise! Now, phonics. Let's read numbers with units. 400 times is four hundred times. At 4,000 meters, the water pushes about 400 times harder than at the surface. 3.8 km is three point eight kilometers. | 1076 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, Does anyone have any questions? Today, Haru has a problem. He wants to say how long giant squid can grow, but he found two different numbers! So we go to the Coral Library with Ms. Emma. Mr. Oswin puts three sources on the big stone table, very, very slowly. He says, Read them all, and then compare them. Haru reads the first two and frowns. Source A says the squid was 12 meters long, but source B says it was 13 meters long. Say it with me. Source A says the squid was 12 meters long, but source B says it was 13 meters long. Great! Let's read all three sources together. "Source A, from a book called Giants of the Sea. In this book, the writer describes a giant squid that scientists measured. It was 12 meters long. Source B, from a card in a sea museum. This giant squid was 13 meters long, from the top of its body to the end of its long tentacles. It was a female. Source C, from Dr. Vale's science magazine. How long can giant squid grow? Scientists are still finding out, because these animals live in the deep sea and are very hard to find. Most of the big ones that scientists have measured are about 10 to 13 meters long. Females are bigger than males." How long was the squid in source B? 13 meters. Which are bigger, males or females? Females. So which source is right? Maybe all of them! Each source may be about a different squid, and different squid can have different sizes. Mr. Oswin nods. Sources can disagree. That's normal. Good researchers say, Let's check more sources. Then they write down where each fact came from. Haru smiles and writes in his notes, The biggest ones measured are about 12 to 13 meters long. Now, phonics. Let's review. Biodiversity means many different kinds of living things. Compare has a strong second part, com-PARE. Every night, pause, they swim up to find food. | 1078 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, We need to protect the places where these animals live. Today, it's time to write our presentation scripts for the Research Festival. Ms. Emma says, Let's practice together first. We'll write one script about Lumi's family, the lanternfish. Then each of you will write your own. Our goal is about 120 words. Our first draft is much too long. One sentence says, Lanternfish are really, really small and they are very, very cute and I like them a lot. Lumi giggles. Thank you, but that's not a fact! So we revise. First, we make it clearer. Second, we add a fact and say where we found it. Third, we cut the extra words. Here is how our talk starts and ends. Today, I'd like to talk about lanternfish. In conclusion, they are small but very important. Say it with me. Today, I'd like to talk about lanternfish. In conclusion, they are small but very important. Great! Let's read our new script. "Today, I'd like to talk about lanternfish. They are small fish that live in the deep sea. Our guide, Lumi, is one of them! First, lanternfish have glowing spots on their bodies. They make their own light. Second, they travel every day. According to a book at the Coral Library, many lanternfish swim up toward the surface at night to eat. Later, they go back down into the dark water. Finally, there are huge numbers of lanternfish in the ocean. Because there are so many of them, they are an important part of life in the sea. Scientists are still finding out more about them. In conclusion, they are small but very important. Thank you very much." Where did we find the fact about swimming up at night? In a book at the Coral Library. How long is our new script? About 120 words. Now, phonics. Let's review. A biography is a book about someone's life. Presentation is strong just before -tion, pres-en-TA-tion. First, pause, it tells us where lanternfish live. | 1080 | `teacherScript` | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In short, we learned how to research, summarize and present. Today is the big day! The Great Dome is full of young researchers, scientists and sea creatures. Ms. Emma and Dr. Vale sit in the front row, and Mr. Oswin sits near the back in his tiny glasses. Lumi swims into the spotlight and says through the shell microphone, Welcome to the Research Festival! Let's find out! Riko talks about how deep-sea fish find food. Haru talks about giant squid. Kiko explains why some animals glow. Birimo talks about sleep, and he says honestly, Scientists are still finding out! Each speaker ends the same way. Thank you for listening. Are there any questions? Say it with me. Thank you for listening. Are there any questions? Wonderful! Let's read the festival program. "Deep Sea City Research Festival. Welcome to the Great Dome! Today, four young researchers from the land above will share what they found out. Riko asks, How do deep-sea fish find food? Haru asks, How big can giant squid grow? Kiko asks, Why do some animals glow? Birimo asks, Do deep-sea animals sleep? After each talk, there will be time for questions, so please think of a good one. Raise your hand and wait for the host. Please clap after every talk. These young researchers read books, checked websites, asked experts and checked their sources. We are very proud of them!" Who are the four speakers? Riko, Haru, Kiko and Birimo. What should everyone do after every talk? Clap. The cheers are loud and warm. Then the sixth pearl appears, and the Lamp of Knowledge shines brightly! Behind us, the old carved door slowly opens. There is a path of glowing pages, and we can hear someone telling a story. Now, phonics. Let's review. Bioluminescent animals make their own light. Research is strong at the end, re-SEARCH. Like a flashlight, pause, some fish can make light. A volcanologist studies volcanoes. 3,800 meters is three thousand eight hundred meters. Our next adventure is in a world of stories! Goodbye, Lumi! Thank you, Deep Sea City! | Hello, everyone! Last time, we said, In short, we learned how to research, summarize and present.
